Evaluation of Dual Language and 
English as a Second Language 
Programs 
KEY FINDINGS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
 
The purpose of this evaluation was to examine (a) program implementation, (b) stakeholder 
perceptions, and (c) program outcomes. 
Key Findings 
The Dual Language/English as a Second Language (DL/ESL1) Department provided supplemental 
assistance to 61,655 Emergent Bilinguals (EBs; 44% of district enrollment) and their families in 2023-24 to 
increase student achievement by effectively addressing the academic, linguistic, and social needs of EBs. 
The DL/ESL Department oversaw DL and ESL instructional programs, sheltered instruction, summer 
programs, and campus Language Proficient Assessment Committees (LPACs) that were responsible for 
the identification and reclassification of EBs and monitoring of reclassified EBs. The reported budget for the 
Bilingual/ESL Program totaled $43,083,242. Key findings include the following: 
 
 
1 For a list of all acronyms and abbreviates used in this evaluation, see Appendix A. 
 
In 2023-24, 52 percent of district students were EBs (n = 71,859), which was a four 
percentage-point increase over two years (2022: 68,396; 2023: 68,751; page 12).  
 
In 2023-24, approximately three-fourths of two-way DL campuses had lower percentages of 
African American opt-in students and economically disadvantaged students than at the campus 
overall (page 12). 
 
DL and ESL staff members who worked with an instructional specialist in 2023-24 generally said 
they were provided with beneficial supports and resources. Rates for ESL were higher than DL 
for almost all supports and resources (page 18). 
 
Most respondents rated all professional development types as helpful, with rates for ESL higher 
than DL on almost all types (page 18). 
 
The DL/ESL Department met all three of their teacher-related survey goals for 2023-24 (page 18). 
 
2024 TELPAS composite rates for EBs at all grade levels clustered in intermediate level of English 
proficiency. For domain rates, EB rates for the speaking and writing domains at the advanced high 
level were significantly lower than the other domains (page 21). 


[PAGE 7]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |2 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2024 TELPAS composite ratings for all grade levels saw increases over one and two years at the 
beginning level and no change or decreases at the advanced and advanced high levels (page 
21). 
 
For all grade levels overall, EBs with both 2023 and 2024 TELPAS rates progressed at higher 
rates than they regressed across the composite and all domains (page 21). 
 
The percentage of EBs that regressed at least one TELPAS level in 2024 (16%) was three 
percentage-points lower than in 2023, which met the DL/ESL Department goal to at least maintain 
the rate of EBs who regressed. In addition, the percentage of EBs that progressed at least one 
TELPAS level in 2024 (31%) was two percentage-points higher than in 2023, which did not meet 
the DL/ESL Department goal of a six percentage-point increase (page 21). 
 
For 2024 Measures of Academic Progress Growth (MAP) beginning-of-year (BOY) to end-of-year 
(EOY) growth quintiles, EBs (a) clustered at the Low quintile on the English mathematics and 
English and Spanish reading subtests and (b) clustered at the High quintile on the Spanish 
mathematics subtest (page 26). 
 
When comparing 2022 and 2023 BOY to EOY quintiles with 2024 BOY to EOY quintiles, the rate 
of EBs in the High quintile (a) stayed the same or trended downward on the English and Spanish 
mathematics subtests and (b) was mixed on the English and Spanish reading subtests (page 26). 
 
EBs did not meet the DL/ESL Department’s three State of Texas Assessments of Academic 
Readiness (STAAR) reading goals (range: 2 percentage points away from grades 6-8 goal to 22 
percentage points away from grade 3 goal; page 29).  
 
From 2023 to 2024, trends for EBs on the mathematics- and reading-related STAAR subtests 
were generally mixed or negative, whereas trends on EOCs were positive at all performance 
standards (page 29). 
 
Overall, monitored students consistently attained the Approaches+, Meets+, and Masters STAAR 
and End-of-Course (EOC) performance standards at considerably higher rates than EBs, 
regardless of the subject (page 32).  
 
Qualified EBs participated in magnet programs at lower rates than NonEBs and monitored 
students (page 36).  
 
When compared to the state and other large, urban Texas districts, Dallas ISD EB performance 
was (a) generally comparable or lower on TELPAS (at Advanced or Advanced High), and (b) were 
generally comparable or higher on STAAR/EOCs (pages 25 and 34). 


[PAGE 8]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |3 
 
 
Recommendations 
Based on the key findings, the following recommendations are offered for stakeholders. 
Continue to examine African American and economically disadvantaged opt-in student 
participation in TWDL programs. In 2023-24, approximately three-fourths of TWDL campuses had lower 
percentages of African American opt-in students (75% of TWDL campuses) and economically 
disadvantaged students (74% of TWDL campuses) than at the campus overall. Efforts should continue to 
educate more NonEB parents about the benefits of TWDL program participation to bring the participation 
percentages more in line with that of the campus overall. 
Continue using DL and ESL specialists in their current roles. In 2023-24, most survey respondents 
expressed positive views regarding the support and resources that they received from DL and ESL 
specialists. For example, between 78 and 97 percent of respondents rated the helpfulness of supports 
provided by the DL and ESL specialist as very or extremely helpful. Additionally, over three-fourths of 
respondents rated the different types of professional development received from DL and ESL specialists 
as helpful. Overall, the DL/ESL Department met all three of their teacher-related survey goals for 2023-24. 
Thus, DL and ESL specialists should continue to provide teachers with effective support. 
Continue efforts to increase EB rates at higher TELPAS proficiency levels. On the 2024 TELPAS 
composite, 10 percent of EBs scored at the advanced high English proficiency level, which was the same 
as 2023 (Voss, 2023). Additionally, composite ratings for all grade levels saw (a) increases over one and 
two years at the beginning level and (b) no change or decreases at the advanced and advanced high levels. 
EB rates for the speaking and writing domains at the advanced high level were significantly lower than the 
other domains. Targeted efforts to improve scores in those domains could lead to increases in the overall 
TELPAS composite ratings. 
Continue focusing on increasing EB achievement on the STAAR performance standards.  
From 2023 to 2024, trends for EBs on the mathematics- and reading-related STAAR subtests were 
generally mixed or negative. Additionally, EBs did not meet the DL/ESL Department’s three STAAR reading 
goals, with the rate of grade three EBs scoring Meets+ (27%) being 22 percentage points lower than the 
goal (49%). Thus, the DL/ESL Department should continue to focus on providing academic support to EBs 
to continue to improve STAAR attainment rates.

English as a Second Language 
Programs 
DEPARTMENT OF EVALUATION AND ASSESSMENT  
PROGRAM BACKGROUND 
The Dallas Independent School District (ISD) Dual Language/English as a Second Language 
(DL/ESL) Department mission is to increase student achievement by effectively addressing the academic, 
linguistic, and social needs of Emergent Bilinguals (EBs)2 through programs that embrace student diversity. 
The department oversees DL and ESL programs, sheltered instruction, services for immigrant students, 
tutoring, and summer programs, and oversees the Language Proficiency Assessment Committee (LPAC), 
which is responsible for the identification, reclassification, and monitoring of EBs. Finally, the department 
collaborates with the Special Education Department to provide services for EBs who require special 
education services (EBSPED). For evaluation of the program serving EBSPED students, see Voss (2024). 
Providing support for dual language programs is important for EBs because it can take six years 
on average for a kindergarten student in a dual language program to meet grade-level academic standards 
in English. For a student who did not receive any prior schooling in their native language, it can take seven 
years or more (Collier & Thomas, 2017). Because of the challenges facing EB students (e.g., length of time 
to meet grade-level standards), there are numerous myths surrounding dual language acquisition, such as 
that bilingual children’s language development and/or academic achievement will be delayed, atypical, or 
incomplete (Genesee, 2015). However, previous research has indicated that developing high levels of 
proficiency in multiple languages does not come at the expense of academic achievement. In fact, when 
students become proficient bilinguals, they often outperform monolingual learners on standardized tests 
(Baker & Wright, 2017). Thus, having a robust and strong DL/ESL Department to help support EBs across 
all grade levels is important for improving outcomes across multiple domains. 
PROGRAM DESCRIPTION 
The sections below include the DL/ESL Program components, people, budget, and program goals. 
For all methodology, see Appendix B. 
Program Components 
Dual Language Programs 
DL programs in the Dallas ISD aimed to promote dual literacy in English and Spanish.  
By participating in DL programs, students learned a second language through content-based instruction 
 
2 To align with state terminology changes made in the 2021-22 school year, Emergent Bilinguals has replaced the term English 
learners (ELs). 


[PAGE 10]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |5 
 
 
while maintaining their dominant languages to promote and embrace cultural diversity among all students. 
Available at most elementary school campuses in 2022-23,3 the Dallas ISD offered three DL program 
models for students:  
• 
One-way DL (OWDL; elementary school level) 
• 
Two-way DL (TWDL; elementary school level) 
• 
Secondary DL (SECDL; middle school and high school levels) 
OWDL 
The OWDL program was a biliteracy program serving identified EBs4 in prekindergarten (PK) to 
grade five (or grade six when housed at an elementary school campus) with academic instruction in English 
and Spanish. Instruction time5 in the OWDL program for PK and K had almost all (90%) content conducted 
in Spanish, with the remaining 10 percent for English Language Development. For first grade, additional 
English content was added [70% Spanish; science, social studies, all of reading/language arts (R/LA)]; 30% 
English: mathematics). Starting in grade two through grade five/six, instruction time was half in Spanish 
(science, social studies, and half of R/LA) and half in English (mathematics and half of R/LA). The one-way 
program was the largest of district DL programs; it was offered at 134 campuses in the Dallas ISD in 
2023-24, which slightly lower than 2022-23 (n = 135). For a list of campuses with an OWDL program, see 
Appendix C. 
TWDL 
The TWDL program was a biliteracy program for PK to grade five (or grade six, when housed at an 
elementary school campus) students with two student populations learning a second language through 
content instruction while maintaining their dominant languages. Ideally, TWDL classrooms were comprised 
of half EBs and half NonEBs. TWDL in the Dallas ISD used the immersion instructional approach, in which 
both dominant English-dominant and Spanish-dominant speaking students learned to read in Spanish first. 
The TWDL immersion program followed the same instructional time guidelines as OWDL. In 2023-24, 
TWDL was offered at 76 campuses in the Dallas ISD, a slight increase from 74 campuses in 2022-23. See 
Appendix D for list of TWDL models by campus. 
Previous research has shown that students are equally capable of achieving academic success in 
either a OWDL or a TWDL program, as long as the program provides high-quality instruction (Thomas & 
Collier, 2012). While TWDL students typically reach grade-level academic standards in English one to two 
years faster than OWDL students, most OWDL and TWDL students meet or exceed grade-level standards 
by the seventh grade (Thomas & Collier, 2012).  
 
3 Nine elementary school level campuses with limited Emergent Bilingual populations lacked DL programs and instead served both 
Spanish- and other language-speaking Emergent Bilinguals through the ESL program. These campuses were Dealey Montessori, 
Pease, Rice, Starks, Stone Montessori, Fridia, Travis Talented and Gifted (TAG), Turner, and Williams TAG. 
4 NonEBs enrolled at campuses without TWDL programs may opt to participate in OWDL programs by parent application in 
kindergarten or grade one if space is available in the program after accommodating all EBs. Spaces available for NonEBs in the 
OWDL program are for one year and are subject to reapplication each year. NonEB students who have received previous DL education 
may apply to the program from grade two to grade five/six, with parental understanding that space availability is not guaranteed each 
year. 
5 In 2022-23, the DL/ESL Department rolled out the Dual Language 2.0 model. For more information on instructional guidelines, see: 
https://drive.google.com/file/d/1r0hvZGd_lfkwgAwQzKTwaQWrV0iRVx9B/view 


[PAGE 11]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |6 
 
 
SECDL 
The SECDL program was a biliteracy pathway with instruction in both English and Spanish that 
provided students in grades six to 12 with the opportunity to graduate with the Seal of Biliteracy.6 All SECDL 
campuses offered at least two courses taught in Spanish from grades six to 12. The SECDL program was 
primarily meant for students continuing from the one- and two-way DL programs. Continuing DL 
programming from the elementary to secondary grade levels can help keep language development and 
learning on consistent and cumulative articulation pathway across the entirety of public schooling (Boyle et 
al., 2015). In 2023-24, 12 campuses (8 middle schools and 4 high schools) offered the SECDL program, 
which was the same as in 2022-23. See Appendix D for list of SECDL campuses. 
English as a Second Language Program 
The purpose of the ESL program was to provide intensive instruction to develop English proficiency 
in listening, reading, speaking, and writing through differentiated instruction and flexible grouping of 
students based upon both linguistic and cognitive levels. EBs at the elementary school grade level for whom 
Spanish was not the dominant language or who were Spanish speakers enrolled at a campus without a DL 
program participated in ESL instruction. In general, ESL programs are beneficial when several native 
languages are represented at a campus, as the model is designed to provide individualized instruction to 
EBs (Kim, Hutchison, and Winsler, 2015). At most campuses, ESL students were placed in classrooms 
with NonEBs.7 ESL at the secondary level was for students in their first three years in United States (US) 
schools. 
Sheltered Instruction 
Secondary students who had been enrolled in US schools for more than three years and had not 
yet met criteria for reclassification were enrolled in sheltered classes for R/LA.8 The objective of sheltered 
instruction was to promote English-language development while providing supports to make grade-level 
standards-based content more accessible to EBs. In the Dallas ISD, most sheltered classrooms included 
both EBs and NonEBs. Sheltered instruction can be helpful for adapting content to meet the needs of 
students linguistically, academically, and culturally, especially for schools with many EBs who speak a 
variety of languages (Marsh, 2018). 
Immigrant Programs 
Students who had been enrolled in US schools fewer than three years were classified as 
immigrants. In 2023-24, approximately 10 percent of Dallas ISD students were immigrants (N = 14,354). 
 
6 The Seal of Biliteracy was awarded by a school, district, or state to recognize students who have demonstrated proficiency in two or 
more languages by graduation from high school. See sealofbiliteracy.org for more information. 
7 At campuses with large populations of ESL students, all teachers were likely to be ESL certified and students could be placed 
anywhere, but ESL students were usually grouped together as much as possible. At campuses with smaller populations of ESL 
students (especially where not all teachers were ESL certified), students tended to be grouped together, but may have shared a 
classroom with NonEBs. 
8 R/LA classes at the secondary level were differentiated by student program levels (beginning, intermediate, advanced, transitional, 
post-transitional) and grade level. Sheltered R/LA courses were typically designed for students at the transitional and post-transitional 
levels. 


[PAGE 12]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |7 
 
 
Because a large proportion of immigrants typically also were EBs (95% in 2023-24), the DL/ESL 
Department played a pivotal role in the integration of immigrant students and their families into the district 
and community. With awarded 2023-24 supplemental Title III funds, the DL/ESL Department aimed to 
(a) provide supplemental instructional resources, (b) assist in the development of tutoring plans for 
improved English proficiency and academic achievement, (c) develop supplemental summer enrichment 
opportunities, (d) connect parents with needed referrals to services, (e) deliver classes for immigrant EBs 
and their parents including district information sessions and ESL classes, and (f) increase parental 
participation in trainings and activities through the Margaret and Gilbert Herrera International Welcome 
Center. 
Immigrants who were new to the country in Kindergarten (K) to grade 12 and had never attended 
US schools were welcomed to the district at the Margaret and Gilbert Herrera International Welcome 
Center. This center supported these students and their families to bolster academic success. Along with 
registration and assessment services, the center provided orientation services for immigrant families as 
they transitioned into the district and community. The intake center also provided follow-up support to 
students through professional development and instructional coaching for teachers of EBs, literacy fairs on 
select Saturdays throughout the year, and summer enrichment programs for newcomer9 students. 
Newcomer Programs 
To prepare immigrant students in the first year in US schools who did not speak Spanish or English 
for academic success, the district also offered a one-year Newcomer Program at designated Dallas ISD 
campuses.10 This program focused on English acquisition and was for students in mixed grade levels. 
Certified teachers provided instructional services to identified students in a multi-age setting using sheltered 
instruction methods and strategies. Newcomer programs are designed to (a) assist immigrant students 
linguistically and academically and (b) help students grow their cross-cultural skills and emphasize family 
engagement (Chu, 2008). Because the students served by the Newcomer Program in 2023-24 were a small 
subsection of immigrant students, they were not highlighted as a separate group in analyses in this report. 
Instead, they were included in results for the general immigrant student population. 
In addition to the Newcomer Program, the DL/ESL Department established the Dallas International 
Academy (DIA), which operated as a school-within-in-a-school. In 2022-23 and 2023-24, DIA was piloted 
at Thomas Jefferson High School, with a plan for the program to be expanded to additional high schools in 
subsequent years. Newcomer EB students could enroll in DIA for grades nine and ten, and they were taught 
by 12 ESL certified and trained teachers in the four core content areas. While the core classes within DIA 
were single population classrooms, newcomer EB students participated in elective courses and extra-
curricular activities with students from across the campus.     
 
9 Newcomer students were recent immigrant students in grades K through grade five who lived outside of the US for two consecutive 
years or more. 
10 Foster Elementary, Herbert Marcus Elementary, McShan Elementary, Anne Frank Elementary, and Gill Elementary. 


[PAGE 13]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |8 
 
 
Language Proficiency Assessment Committee  
Each campus in the Dallas ISD operated an LPAC serving as a liaison between home and campus 
and as an advocate for EBs. The responsibilities of LPACs included (a) identification, placement, 
assessment, and document review; (b) instructional methodologies and/or interventions; (c) annual review 
of student progress; (d) linguistic accommodation and assessment decisions; (e) parental notification; and 
(f) maintaining updated and accurate documentation. LPAC membership was required to at least consist 
of a principal or assistant principal, a bilingual certified or ESL certified teacher who worked directly with 
EBs, and a non-faculty parent of a currently enrolled EB student. The LPAC was required to meet at least 
six documented times per academic year, ideally every six weeks.11  
Identification Process  
The identification and placement of identified EBs had to be completed within 4512 school days of 
the 2023-24 school year.13 The LPAC was responsible for this process, using criteria mandated by the 
Texas Education Agency (TEA). A parent-signed and dated home language survey requesting the primary 
language spoken at home and the most frequently spoken language by the student was administered to all 
incoming students.14 If the response was a language other than English for either question, the student was 
considered a potential EB. The student underwent English language proficiency, Spanish language 
proficiency (Spanish speakers only), and norm-referenced language assessments to determine whether 
EB designation was required.15 Before placing an EB student in a program, a parent had to sign and date 
a notification letter. If parent permission for enrollment was not secured, explicit parental denial had to be 
documented in writing. Identified EB students whose parents deny services (parent denials) were placed in 
general education and were ineligible for specialized language supports. An identification flowchart is 
provided in Appendix E. 
Reclassification Process 
Prior to 2020-21, per district policy, EB students in PK to grade four were ineligible for 
reclassification from EB to NonEB status. Starting last year, beginning at the end of K through grade 12, 
the LPAC could evaluate students for possible reclassification at the end of each academic year. EBs who 
were under consideration for reclassification were required to meet criteria demonstrating English mastery 
in the realms of listening, speaking, reading, writing, and comprehension, and had to be able to participate 
equally with NonEBs in general education classrooms. EBs recommended for reclassification were 
monitored every six weeks at regular LPAC meetings for four academic years after reclassification. 
Grade-level details of reclassification criteria are provided in Appendix E. 
Monitoring of Reclassified EBs 
 
11 For an LPAC meeting to be considered official, at least the minimally required members must be present. 
12 The identification window is usually within 20 school days of initial student enrollment. However, due to delays to 2022-23 TELPAS 
and STAAR scores, the window was extended to 45 days for both new enrollees and returning students for this year only. 
13 Missing this deadline may impact staffing and funding for a campus, and potentially may negatively affect student success.   
14 The home language survey is completed for a student once during his/her educational career. This document is a legal document 
that cannot be changed. 
15 These assessments were conducted as appropriate for grade level. 


[PAGE 14]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |9 
 
 
Along with monitoring all EBs, campus LPACs reviewed the academic progress, grades, and 
assessment data of reclassified EBs every six weeks during regularly scheduled meetings. In the event of 
any course failures, the committee met with general education teachers providing instruction to determine 
if the lack of progress was because of limited language proficiency or other factors (e.g., attendance, 
incomplete assignments). If attributed to limited English proficiency, the LPAC developed an instructional 
intervention plan in conjunction with the applicable general education teacher(s). 
Program Staff 
In order to assist EBs in the Dallas ISD, Title III funds16 helped support DL/ESL program leadership 
and support positions. Title III ELA funds allowed the DL/ESL Department to employ (a) 20 instructional 
specialists to assist elementary, middle, and high schools, (b) seven coordinators to assist elementary, 
middle, and high schools, (c) one coordinator to assist refugee families and students, (d) three parent 
coordinators to assist with parent activities, and (e) two assistant parent coordinators to assist with parent 
activities. 
Budget 
The reported budget for the Dual Language/ESL and Immigrant Programs totaled $35,001,715: 
$8,614,775 from Title III, Part A EB funds,17 and $26,386,940 from general operating funds.18 
Program Goals 
1. The percentage of EBs who regressed on each Texas English Language Proficiency Assessment 
System (TELPAS) domain will not increase from 2023 to 2024.19 (Yes, goal met; see page 21 for 
more detail). 
2. The percentage of EBs who progressed or maintained Advanced High on their TELPAS composite 
level will increase by six percentage points from 2023 to 2024. (No, goal not met, see page 21 for 
more detail).

EVALUATION QUESTIONS AND RESULTS21 
EQ1: How was the DL/ESL Program implemented in 2023-24? 
Population Served 
Teachers Served 
This section summarizes the characteristics of teachers in DL/ESL classrooms22 and the number 
of exceptions and waivers submitted to the state for those lacking required certifications. Characteristics of 
elementary and secondary ESL and DL teachers are provided in Table 1.  
• 
At the elementary level, 2,486 teachers instructed in ESL and DL classrooms and 78 teachers 
instructed in ESL and SECDL classrooms at the secondary level.  
• 
For both elementary and secondary teachers overall, the highest percentages of teachers were 
female, Hispanic, held bachelor’s degrees, and/or had been with the district between one and five 
years.  
• 
The rate of 2023-24 elementary-level teachers with a Proficient I or above TEI effectiveness level 
was 62 percent for ESL teachers and 66 percent for DL teachers (69% for elementary-level district 
teachers overall).  
• 
The rate of secondary-level teachers with a Proficient I or above TEI effectiveness level was 43 
percent for ESL teachers, and 64 percent for DL teachers (61% for secondary-level district teachers 
overall).  
• 
In addition to the SECDL and ESL teachers, all secondary R/LA teachers were considered 
sheltered teachers. In 2023-24, the district employed 928 secondary R/LA teachers.

Exceptions and Waivers. The TEA requires that districts unable to provide the required number 
of bilingual certified teachers to implement the bilingual program or ESL certified teachers to implement the 
ESL program must request from the commissioner of education an exception to offer an alternative program 
(bilingual) or a waiver of the certification requirements for teachers (ESL).23  
In 2023-24, the number of bilingual certified teachers was 1,478, which was lower than 2021-22 
and 2022-23 (n = 1,757 and 1,875, respectively; Table 2). This decrease corresponded with a slight 
increase in the number bilingual exceptions in 2023-24 (+17). In addition, while the number of ESL certified 
teachers trended upward from 2021-22 to 2023-24 (n = 599 to 836), the number of requested ESL waivers 
fluctuated during this time (n = 66 to 113). 
 
 
23 The details regarding certification requirements are beyond the scope of this document. For more information, see the Bilingual 
Education Exception and ESL Waiver Resources information on https://tea.texas.gov/academics/special-student-populations/english-
learner-support/bilingual-education-exception-and-esl-waiver-resources.

Key Findings from this section: 
 
In 2023-24, 52 percent of district students were EBs (n = 71,859), which was a four 
percentage-point increase over two years (2022: 68,396; 2023: 68,751). 
 
In 2023-24, approximately three-fourths of TWDL campuses had lower percentages of African 
American and economically disadvantaged opt-in students than at the campus overall.

students in 2023-24. At least part of decreased counts of reclassified students may be attributed to the rigor 
of reclassification requirements.

Student Enrollment by Program/Classification. As shown in Table 3, 52 percent of district 
students were EBs in 2023-24, which was a four percentage-point increase compared to the past two years.  
The greatest number of EBs served by the DL/ESL Department in 2023-24 were those enrolled in OWDL 
(n = 30,526; 22% of district), followed by sheltered students (n = 22,475; 16% of district). From 2021-22 to 
2023-24, the largest two-year increase in the percentage of the district population for students in DL/ESL 
programs/classifications was for All EBs (+4 percentage points) followed by Immigrant students  
(+3 percentage points). The largest two-year percentage point decrease was for NonEBs (-4 percentage 
points).

Student Characteristics. Grade level breakdowns for EBs (overall and by years identified), 
NonEBs, and monitored students (overall and by year of monitoring) are provided in Appendix F.  
Appendix G provides detailed student characteristics by program/classification and grade level. The highest 
numbers of monitored students in the district were in grades 12 (n = 257) and 11 (n = 197), and the rates 
of monitored district students by year of monitoring were highest in the first year (45%). 
EB characteristics for the elementary school and secondary school grade levels were relatively 
comparable, except for the higher rate of elementary EBs born in countries other than the US (42%; 
secondary: 27%). Differences between EBs and NonEBs were mostly as expected, with differences mainly 


[PAGE 20]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |15 
 
 
in characteristics that are often associated with EB status. These included having higher rates of EBs than 
NonEBs classified as: Hispanic, at risk, economically disadvantaged, speaking Spanish at home, and 
having a birth country other than the United States. The largest number (n = 29,955) of 2023-24 district EB 
students were enrolled for at least the seventh year as an EB,26 and most of these students were in grades 
six to eight (36%) or grades nine to 12 (49%).  
Of the 76 campuses with TWDL programs,27 57 campuses (75%) had lower percentages of African 
American opt-in students in TWDL than at the campus overall (Appendix H). In a similar pattern,  
56 campuses (74%) had lower percentages of economically disadvantaged opt-in students in TWDL than 
rates of economically disadvantaged students at the campus overall. Thus, opt-in students in TWDL were 
less likely to be African American or economically disadvantaged than the general population of the TWDL 
campuses. Ideally, opt-in TWDL participation would mirror the demographic make-up of each campus. 
Activities Implemented 
This section provides summaries of activities implemented by the DL/ESL Department in 2023-24. 
Planned activities include (a) supplemental professional development, (b) summer enrichment programs, 
(c) engagement in parent workshops and trainings, (d) supplemental resources, (e) the DL Cadre and ESL 
Leadership Cohort, and (f) the International Welcome Center. 
Supplemental Professional Development28 
A total of 8,387 staff (DL: 5,184; ESL: 3,203) attended DL- and/or ESL-related professional 
development sessions in 2023-24.  There were 270 districtwide sessions (DL: 72; ESL: 198) provided 
throughout the year.  
Summer Enrichment Programs 
In Summer 2024, the DL/ESL Department conducted five student summer programs: Science 
Discovery Camp, Spanish Immersion Camp, DestiNations Language Enrichment Camp, ESL Summer 
Explorers Camp, and Accelerate. Overall, the DL/ESL Department met their goal of 800 students 
participating in 2024 summer programs (n = 1,038). Additionally, the DL/ESL Department conducted two 
parent summer programs: the ESL Parent Summer Enrichment Program and Parent Technology Classes. 
Science Discovery Camp.  The Science Discovery Camp allowed DL students to expand their 
understanding of key science concepts in both English and Spanish and engage in interactive lessons, 
labs, and field trips. In Summer 2024, the camp was held at five campuses from June 3 to June 20, 2024. 
A total of 297 students attended at least one day of camp. The highest percentages of students were 
Hispanic (99%), economically disadvantaged (91%), and/or were considered at risk (98%). 
 
26 Years since enrollment date was calculated as a proxy for the current year of EB status. Available demographic files provided a 
‘Years LEP’ field that included data for approximately 92 percent of current EBs. At the suggestion of department leadership, the 
evaluator calculated a new field that used the original enrollment date and the 2023 PEIMS date to calculate the year of EB status for 
each current EB student. Because students must be tested for EB status within the first 45 days of enrollment in the district, this 
methodology provided a sound approximation of Years in EB status for most students 
27 School for the Talented and Gifted in Pleasant Grove and Alex Sanger Preparatory School included both elementary and secondary 
grade levels. 
28 The agreed upon window of time for reporting professional development was 07/01/21 to 06/30/22 to allow for timely publication of 
this information in the evaluation report while including most professional development offered for a given year. 


[PAGE 21]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |16 
 
 
Spanish Immersion Camp. The Science Immersion Camp provided interactive DL experience for 
DL opt-in students in grades two through five by allowing them to strengthen their language skills through 
activities grounded in science concepts, integrated in learning labs, and embedded into field trips.  
In Summer 2024, the Spanish Immersion Camp was held at five campuses from June 3 to  
June 20, 2024. A total of 86 students attended at least one day of the camp. Furthermore, the highest 
percentages of students were female (58%) and/or Hispanic (71%), and approximately two-thirds of the 
students participated in talented and gifted programs (70%). 
DestiNations Language Enrichment Camp. The DestiNations Language Enrichment Camp 
targeted grades K to five newcomer EB students in the first three years in the United States. This program 
provided engaging, hands-on activities, as well as opportunities to build proficiency in the English language 
by making connections through science, social studies, RLA, and mathematics content. In Summer 2024, 
the DestiNations Language Enrichment Camp was held at five elementary schools from June 3 through 
June 20, 2024. A total of 306 students attended at least one day of this program. Most students were female 
(54%), Hispanic (88%), and/or economically disadvantaged (83%). 
ESL Summer Explorers Camp. The ESL Summer Explorers Camp (formerly T-CALL) targeted 
grades six to ten newcomer EB students in the first three years in the United States. This program focused 
on linguistic development through learning experiences and project-based learning. In Summer 2024, the 
ESL Summer Explorers Camp was held at four middle schools from June 3 to June 20, 2024. A total of 250 
students attended at least one day of this program. Most students were female (60%), Hispanic (84%), 
and/or economically disadvantaged (74%). 
Accelerate. The Accelerate Program offered the opportunity for high school EBs to accelerate their 
learning and acquire high school graduation credits in courses like English ESOL1, Government and 
Economics, Geometry, and Physics. In Summer 2024, the Credit Acceleration Program was held virtually 
for all high school EBs from June 3 to July 11, 2024. A total of 99 students participated in at least one day 
of this program. Most students were male (61%), Hispanic (60%) or Asian (24%), economically 
disadvantaged (82%), and/or were considered at-risk (86%). 
ESL Parent Summer Enrichment Program. The ESL Parent Summer Enrichment Program was 
held virtually for parents of EB students. The program, divided into three language levels, provided 22 hours 
of instruction to help teach parents the English language through practice with vocabulary, grammar skills, 
and basic conversation. In Summer 2024, the ESL Parent Summer Enrichment Program was held from 
June 3 to June 20, 2024. Overall, 276 parents completed the program. 
Parent Technology Classes. The Parent Technology Classes targeted parents of EBs to help 
them learn about technology using different applications and education sites. The program was designed 
to empower parents to help reinforce their child’s learning skills at home and support their academic process 
and language acquisition. In Summer 2024, the Parent Technology Classes were held at two elementary 
schools from June 3 to June 18, 2024. A total of 57 parents or guardians attended. 


[PAGE 22]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |17 
 
 
Parent Workshop/Training Engagement 
In 2023-24, 50 parents attended Family Literacy Fair sessions.29 All parents completed parent 
survey administered by the DL/ESL Department at the end of the Family Literacy Fair sessions.  
Most parents (95%) agreed that attending the sessions motivated them to be more involved in the education 
of their children, which exceeded the DL/ESL Department goal of 93 percent agreement. 
Supplemental Resources 
During 2023-24, the DL/ESL Department distributed resources and materials to EB students30 and 
those involved with helping EBs, such as parents31 and teachers.32  
DL Cadre/ESL Leadership Cohort 
The DL Cadre was a large DL teacher leadership group focused on improving teaching, building 
knowledge, and providing professional development throughout the summer. They also helped run summer 
programs. To join the DL Cadre, teachers with three or more years of DL certified teaching experience had 
to be (a) recommended by a DL stakeholder, (b) apply, (c) interview with the Cadre committee, (d) present 
a DL PowerPoint to the Cadre committee, and (e) translate/adapt a selected text to be evaluated. Teachers 
were unable to participate in summer DL/ESL professional development, present DL/ESL professional 
development, or work at the Science Discovery Camp unless they were accepted. The DL Cadre met 
multiple times throughout the year. 
The ESL Leadership Cohort was similar to the DL Cadre, though smaller and dedicated to ESL and 
sheltered teachers. Teachers were invited to apply by DL/ESL staff leadership or instructional specialists 
at campuses. The ESL Leadership Cohort participated in approximately five Saturday sessions a year and 
focused on increasing pedagogical content knowledge. 
In the 2023-24 school year, the DL Cadre accepted four new teachers, for a total of  
28 participating members. The ESL Leadership Cohort was comprised of 15 new teachers in 2023-24. 
International Welcome Center 
In 2023-24, 5,115 parents attended Parent Orientation sessions at the International Welcome 
Center, which was a one-year attendance increase of 2,338 parents (2022-23: 2,777 parents). A total of 
2,216 parents overall completed parent surveys administered by the DL/ESL Department via the 
International Welcome Center at Parent Orientation sessions. Most parents (99%) agreed that the services 
they received during orientation were excellent, which met the department’s goal of 98 percent. 
 
29 For Family Literacy Fairs, participating teachers helped run stations for children and their parents focused on various literacy topics 
(e.g., story elements, sequencing, reading fluency). 
30 Provided materials to students included backpacks, digital and physical books, software licenses, headsets, educational games, 
manipulatives, dictionaries, and workbooks. 
31 Provided materials to parents included school supply materials, software licenses, parent backpacks with supplies, and Adult ESL 
Classes books. 
32 Provided materials to teachers included software licenses and instructional books.

EQ2: What were the perceptions of DL/ESL staff members?33 
This section includes key results from the DL/ESL survey.34 Full results by question, respondent 
type, and response frequency are provided in Appendix I.  
Respondent Characteristics 
The highest percentage of DL staff members worked with PK to grade two (62%) and grade three 
to five students (35%), whereas the highest percentage of ESL staff worked with grade six to eight (53%) 
and grade 9 to 12 students (46%). The highest percentage of DL and ESL staff members reported meeting 
with an instructional specialist more than 10 times (DL: 41%; ESL: 68%). 
Benefit of Instructional Specialists Supports 
Most DL and ESL staff members who responded to the survey reported that instructional specialists 
provided beneficial supports during the 2023-24 school year. For example, a high percentage of DL and 
ESL staff members rated both in-person support (DL: 92%; ESL: 90%) and virtual support (DL: 83%;  
ESL: 86%) as very or extremely beneficial. In addition, as shown in Figure 3, most ESL and DL staff 
members rated each support provided by the instructional specialists as very or extremely beneficial. 
The percentage of ESL staff members who rated the supports as very or extremely beneficial ranged from 
83 percent (lesson planning support) to 97 percent (providing resources/materials), and DL staff members 
ranged from 78 percent (lesson planning support) to 95 percent (providing resources/materials).  
 
 
33 Of the 165 DL and 96 ESL staff members who received an invitation to complete the online survey, 84 (51%) DL and 59 (61%) ESL 
staff members completed at least half of the survey. Results were combined for teachers, administrators, and CICs. 
34 For questions related to DL/ESL instructional specialist interactions, percentages do not include respondents who indicated that a 
question was not applicable (e.g., they did not receive support from a(n) DL/ESL specialist) or that they did not know or were not sure 
how to answer a question. 
Key Findings from this section: 
 
DL and ESL staff members who worked with an instructional specialist in 2023-24 generally said 
they were provided with beneficial supports and resources. Rates for ESL were higher than DL 
for almost all supports and resources. 
 
Most respondents rated all professional development types as helpful, with rates for ESL higher 
than DL on almost all types. 
 
The DL/ESL Department met all three of their teacher-related survey goals for 2023-24.

Resources Provided by Instructional Specialists 
Generally, resources provided by instructional specialists were rated as beneficial by a high 
percentage of respondents. The percentage of DL staff members who rated resources as very or extremely 
beneficial ranged from 86 percent (curriculum products) to 91 percent (supplies and resources for 
classroom implementation), and the percentage of ESL staff members ranged from 91 percent (online 
resources) to 96 percent (curriculum products). 
Professional Development and DL/ESL Curriculum 
Over three-fourths of DL staff members rated the different professional development types as very 
or extremely beneficial. The percentage ranged from 77 percent (campus-wide professional development) 
to 87 percent (one-on-one). The percentage of ESL staff members ranged from 81 percent (campus-wide 
professional development and PLCs) to 100 percent (summer institutes). 
In addition, most DL and ESL staff members indicated that the DL or ESL curriculum was very or 
extremely effective at meeting the needs of EBSPED students (DL: 64%; ESL: 72%).

Program Goals for DL/ESL Survey 
The DL/ESL Department met all three of their teacher survey department goals for 2023-24: 
• 
88 percent of teachers rated campus support and professional development as very or extremely 
beneficial in 2023-24 (Goal: 88%; 2022-23: 84%).  
• 
90 percent of teachers rated all resources as very or extremely beneficial in 2023-24 (Goal: 88%; 
2022-23: 85%).  
• 
91 percent of ESL teachers rated the supports of PD, in-class support, and providing 
resources/materials as very or extremely beneficial (Goal: 75%; 2022-23: 74%). 
EQ3: What were the academic outcomes for EBs during 2023-24?35 
This section includes summaries of academic outcomes for EBs in 2023-24, including TELPAS, 
Measures of Academic Progress Growth (MAP), STAAR, and EOC.    
TELPAS English Proficiency Ratings,36 Level Movement,37 and Trends  
The following TELPAS data are available in the appendices: 
• 
Appendix J: English proficiency composite rating levels for 2022, 2023, and 2024 by 
program/classification and grade level, 
• 
Appendix K: Composite level movement rates from 2023 to 2024 for each grouping, 
• 
Appendix L: TELPAS English proficiency domain rates for 2024 and level movement rates from 
2023 to 2024 for each program/classification, 
• 
Appendix T: TELPAS English proficiency composite rates for long-term EBs. 
 
35 Prior to 2023-24, the PEIMS demographic file from the beginning of the school year was used to identify eligible student groups for 
the DL/ESL Program evaluation. Starting in 2023-24, eligibility was determined using an end-of-year (EOY) demographic file. In 
addition, to more accurately reflect which students participated in the program in 2023-24, analyses for outcome measures (e.g., MAP 
Growth, TELPAS, and STAAR) only included students in the EOY demographic file who had been enrolled at least 85 percent of 
possible enrollment days during the school year. 
36 For more information about TELPAS, see: https://tea.texas.gov/student-assessment/testing/telpas/telpas-resources. 
37 EBs who received English proficiency level composite and/or domain ratings for both school years were included in analyses, and 
the evaluator calculated the number and percentage of students who progressed, stayed the same, or regressed at least one level 
among all composite and domain ratings between 2023 and 2024 for which the students had both scores. Students were considered 
to have progressed if they improved by at least one composite level from the prior year to the current year, or if they scored Advanced 
High both years. 


[PAGE 26]
2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |21 
 
 
Dallas ISD EBs 
TELPAS ratings.  
Composite rates. As shown in Table 5 (page 23), 2024 TELPAS composite ratings for EBs at all 
grade levels tended to cluster in the intermediate (range: 41%, grades K to 5 and 6 to 8 to 47%, grades 9 
to 12) level of English proficiency. Additionally, 10 percent of EBs at all grade levels scored at the advanced 
high English proficiency level, which was the same as 2023 (Voss, 2023).  
Domain ratings. Domain rates for EBs at the advanced high level for speaking (range: 2% to 6%) 
and writing (range: 4% to 16%) were significantly lower than for listening (range: 23% to 32%) and reading 
(range: 15% to 30%). Generally, grade level differences were most pronounced for the listening, reading, 
and writing domains, with the youngest students clustering at the lower proficiency levels.  
TELPAS ratings trends.  
Composite rating trends. One- and two-year trends38 in TELPAS composite English proficiency 
rates varied and ranged from the largest decrease for grades nine to 12 over two years at the advanced 
level (-8 percentage points) to the largest increase in grades K to five over two years at the beginning level 
(+7 percentage points). Overall, all grade levels saw increases over one and two years at the beginning 
level and no change or decreases at the advanced and advanced high levels. 
Domain rating trends. One- and two-year changes in domain proficiency rates for EBs varied and 
ranged from the largest decrease for grades six to eight in writing over two years at the advanced high level 
(-27 percentage points) to the largest increase in grades six to eight in writing over two years at the 
intermediate level (+19 percentage points). Almost all grade levels saw increases over one and two years 
at the beginning level. Additionally, the largest decreases at advanced high occurred over two years in the 
writing domain (-14 percentage points). 
 
38 One-year trends reflect percentage-point change from 2023 to 2024. Two-year trends reflect percentage-point change from 2022 
to 2024. 
Key Findings from this section: 
 
2024 TELPAS composite rates for EBs at all grade levels clustered in intermediate level of English 
proficiency. For domain rates, EB rates for the speaking and writing domains at the advanced high 
level were significantly lower than the other domains. 
 
2024 TELPAS composite ratings for all grade levels saw increases over one and two years at the 
beginning level and no change or decreases at the advanced and advanced high levels. 
 
For all grade levels overall, EBs with both 2023 and 2024 TELPAS rates progressed at higher 
rates than they regressed across the composite and all domains. 
 
The rate of EBs that regressed at least one TELPAS level in 2024 (16%) was three percentage-
points lower than 2023, which met the goal to maintain the rate who regressed. In addition, the 
rate of EBs that progressed at least one TELPAS level in 2024 (31%) was two percentage-points 
higher than 2023, which did not meet the goal of a six percentage-point increase.

2 0 2 3 - 2 4  E V A L UA TI O N OF  D UA L L A NG U A G E  A N D  E S L P R O G RA M S  |23 
 
TELPAS level movement.39  
Composite level movement. In 2024, between 52 and 56 percent of EBs remained at the same 
composite proficiency level. Additionally, EBs in grades one to five progressed at a higher rate (grades 1 
to 5: 33%; 6 to 8: 26%; 9 to 12: 25%; Table 6, page 24) and regressed at a lower rate (grades 1 to 5: 10%; 
6 to 8: 18%; 9 to 12: 23%) than those in the middle and upper grades. For all grade levels overall, EBs 
progressed at higher rates (31%) than they regressed (16%). 
Domain level movement. Across all domains and grades, 28 to 64 percent of EBs remained at the 
same proficiency level in 2024. For all grade levels overall, EBs progressed at higher rates across all 
domains (range: 22% to 53%) than they regressed (range: 7% to 28%). 
TELPAS level movement trends.  
Composite level movement trends. For one-year trends40 in TELPAS level movement, composite 
progression rate changes ranged from a one percentage-point increase (grades 9 to 12) to a three 
percentage-point increase (grades 1 to 5). For two-year trends41 in TELPAS level movement, composite 
progression rate changes ranged from a seven percentage-point decrease (grades 9 to 12) to a three 
percentage-point decrease (grades 1 to 5).  
Domain level movement trends. From 2023 to 2024, overall progression rates for domains were 
higher for the writing domain (+9 percentage points) and slightly lower for the listening (-1 percentage point), 
speaking (-2 percentage point) and reading (-1 percentage point) domains, whereas regression rates saw 
were lower for the writing (-22 percentage points) domain, saw no change for the listening and speaking 
domains, and higher for the reading (+2 percentage points) domain. Over two years, overall progression 
rates for domains saw no change for the reading domain and was lower for the speaking (-3 percentage 
points), speaking (7 percentage points), and writing (-16 percentage points) domains, whereas regression 
rates were the same or lower for the listening (-1 percentage point) and reading (no change) domains and 
higher for the speaking (+3 percentage points) and writing (+1 percentage point) domains. Additionally, the 
largest decreases in progression rates occurred in the writing domain over two years (-16 percentage 
points). 
Program Goals. In 2024, Dallas ISD EBs met one of the two DL/ESL Department goals regarding 
TELPAS movement: 
• 
The percentage of EBs that regressed at least one TELPAS level in 2024 (16%) was three 
percentage-points lower than in 2023, which met the DL/ESL Department goal to at least maintain 
the rate of EBs who regressed. 
• 
The percentage of EBs that progressed at least one TELPAS level in 2024 (31%) was two 
percentage-points higher than in 2023, which did not meet the DL/ESL Department goal of a six 
percentage-point increase.

40 One-year trends reflect level movement change from 2023 to 2024 (2024) as compared to level movement change from 2022 to 
2023 (2023). 
41 Two-year trends reflect level movement change from 2023 to 2024 (2024) as compared to level movement change from 2021 to 
2022 (2022).

Dallas ISD vs State and Other Large, Urban Texas District EBs42  
TELPAS composite ratings. As shown in Table 7, rates of EBs in Dallas ISD performing at the 
higher English proficiency levels on the 2024 TELPAS were lower than state rates, especially for the lower 
grades. At the highest English proficiency levels, differences between Dallas ISD and state attainment rates 
ranged from 10 percentage points below state rates (grades K to 2, Advanced) to four percentage points 
below state rates (grades 3 to 12, Advanced High).

Key Findings from this section: 
 
Rates of Dallas ISD students performing at the higher English proficiency levels on the 2024

MAP Growth43 
Detailed MAP Growth data from BOY to EOY by program/classification, grade level, test language, 
and subject for 2022, 2023, and 2024 are provided in Appendix M.  
BOY to EOY Growth Quintile44 
Table 9 shows the 2024 percentages of students from each BOY to EOY growth quintile on the 
mathematics and reading subtests, as well as one- and two-year trends.  
On the mathematics subtest, BOY to EOY growth rates for EBs on the English version tended to 
cluster in the Low quintile (range: 25% to 35%) at all grade levels, whereas EBs on the Spanish version 
tended to cluster in the High quintile (range: 31% to 42%). 
• 
For the English mathematics subtest from 2022 to 2024, except for grade one, the rate of EBs in 
the Low quintile trended upward over one and two years (range: +1 to +5 percentage points), 
whereas the rate in the High quintile stayed the same or trended downward over one and two years 
(no change to -8 percentage points).  
• 
For the Spanish mathematics subtest from 2022 to 2024, the rate of EBs in the Low quintile trended 
upward over one year (+2 to +17 percentage points) and were mixed over two years. Except for 
grade one, the rate of EBs in the High quintile trended downward over one and two years (-25 to -4 
percentage points).  
On the reading subtest, BOY to EOY growth rates for EBs tended to cluster in the Low quintile at 
all grade levels for the English version (range: 30% to 35%) and Spanish version (range: 23% to 46%).  
• 
For the English reading subtest from 2022 to 2024, the rate of EBs in both the Low quintile (range: 
-8 to +6 percentage points) and the High quintile (range: -4 to +2 percentage points) did not show 
a consistent trend over one and two years.  
• 
For the Spanish reading subtest from 2022 to 2024, the rate of EBs in both the Low quintile (range: 
-12 to +17 percentage points) and the High quintile (range: -2 to +3 percentage points) did not show 
a consistent trend over one and two years.
 
43 Prior to 2023-24, the PEIMS demographic file was used to determine eligible students in the DL/ESL Program evaluations. In order 
to more accurately reflect which students participated in the program throughout the school year, 2023-24 data used an EOY 
demographic file that only included students who had been enrolled for 85 percent of possible enrollment days. 
44 BOY to EOY Growth Quintiles: Low: up to the 20th percentile; Low Average: 21st to 40th percentile; Average: 41st to 60th percentile; 
High Average: 61st to 80th percentile; High: 81st percentile and above. 
Key Findings from this section: 
 
For 2024 MAP BOY to EOY growth quintiles, EBs (a) clustered at the Low quintile on the English 
mathematics and the English and Spanish reading subtests and (b) clustered at the High quintile 
on the Spanish mathematics subtest.  
 
When comparing 2022 and 2023 BOY to EOY quintiles with 2024 BOY to EOY quintiles, the rate 
of EBs in the High quintile (a) stayed the same or trended downward on the English and Spanish 
mathematics subtests and (b) was mixed on the English and Spanish reading subtests.

Dallas ISD EBs

reading-related STAAR and EOC subtests can be found in Table 10. Because of changes to the STAAR 
and EOC tests in 2023, comparisons to previous years should be made with caution. 
Mathematics-related subtests. For mathematics, rates of all EBs at the Approaches+ standard 
ranged from 64 percent (grades 6-8) to 78 percent (Algebra I), rates at the Meets+ standard ranged from  
31 percent (grades 6-8) to 40 percent (grades 3-5), and rates at the Masters standard ranged from eight 
 
45 Academic performance standards represent the degree to which students have mastered content as measured by test scores. More 
information on STAAR performance standards: http://tea.texas.gov/student.assessment/staar/performance-standards/. STAAR and 
EOC assessment scores are categorized into four levels to describe student performance: (a) Does Not Meet Grade Level (Does Not 
Meet), (b) Approaches+, (c) Meets+, and (d) Masters. Cut scores divide student scores into these four categories. 
46 2022 results represent STAAR and EOC administrations occurring during the 2021-22 school year, 2023 results represent STAAR 
and EOC administrations occurring during the 2022-23 school year, and 2024 results represent STAAR and EOC administrations 
occurring during the 2023-24 school year. 
47 Prior to 2023-24, the PEIMS demographic file was used to determine eligible students in the DL/ESL Program evaluations. In order

Key Findings from this section: 
 
EBs did not meet the DL/ESL Department’s three STAAR reading goals (range: 2 percentage 
points away from grades 6-8 goal to 22 percentage points away from grade 3 goal). 
 
From 2023 to 2024, trends for EBs on the mathematics- and reading-related STAAR subtests 
were generally mixed or negative, whereas trends on EOCs were positive at all performance 
standards.

Monitored Students 
2022, 2023, and 2024 rates of Monitored students at Approaches+, Meets+, and Masters on the 
mathematics- and reading-related STAAR and EOC subtests can be found in Table 11. Because of 
changes to the STAAR and EOC tests in 2023, comparisons to previous years should be made with caution. 
Mathematics-related subtests. For mathematics, rates of monitored students at the Approaches+ 
standard ranged from 90 percent (Math 3-5) to 98 percent (Math 6-8), rates at the Meets+ standard ranged 
from 64 percent (grades 3-5) to 83 percent (grades 6-8), and rates at the Masters standard ranged from  
39 percent (grades 3-5) to 57 percent (Algebra I). From 2023 to 2024, with one exception, monitored student 
rate trends on all mathematics-related subtests were positive, ranging from a three percentage-point 
increase (grades 3-5, Masters) to a 25 percentage-point increase (Algebra I, Masters). 
Reading-related subtests. For reading, rates of monitored students at the Approaches+ standard 
ranged from 84 percent (grades 3-5) to 99 percent (grades 6-8), rates at the Meets+ standard ranged from 
76 percent (grades 3-5) to 98 percent (grades 6-8), and rates at the Masters standard ranged from 31 
percent (English II) to 84 percent (grades 6-8). From 2023 to 2024, monitored student rate trends on all 
reading-related subtests were positive, ranging from a two percentage-point increase (grades 6-8 and 
English II, Approaches+) to a 28 percentage-point increase (English I, Masters).
Key Findings from this section: 
 
Overall, monitored students consistently attained the Approaches+, Meets+, and Masters STAAR 
and EOC performance standards at considerably higher rates than EBs, regardless of the subject.

Dallas ISD vs State and other large, urban Texas district EBs 
As shown in Table 12, Dallas ISD EBs generally performed better than the state on STAAR reading,

performance standard attainment rates for EBs ranged from 12 percentage points below state rates (grade 
3 mathematics Approaches+ in Spanish) to 12 percentage points above state rates (grade 5 reading 
Meets+ in Spanish). Because few EBs take the Spanish mathematics exam in the Dallas ISD, those findings 
should be considered with caution.

Table 13 shows the rate of EBs in the Dallas ISD, the state, and other large, urban Texas on the 
mathematics- and reading-related 2024 STAAR and EOC subtests. Dallas ISD EBs performed very well on 
STAAR and EOCs compared to other large, urban Texas ISDs and the state. At all performance standards, 
Dallas ISD EBs scores were comparable or higher than the state and all regional comparison groups, with 
one exception.  
• 
Dallas ISD EBs in STAAR 3-8 ranged from one percentage point below comparison rates (reading, 
Approaches+, state) to 18 percentage points above comparison rates (mathematics; Approaches+, 
Austin ISD and San Antonio ISD).  
• 
Dallas ISD rates on EOCs ranged from two percentage points above comparison rates (Algebra I, 
Approaches+, El Paso ISD and English II, Masters, state) to 22 percentage points above comparison 
rates (Algebra I, Meets+, Austin ISD and Fort Worth ISD). 
 
 
Key Findings from this section: 
 
In 2024, Dallas ISD STAAR and EOC rates were generally comparable to or higher than all 
regional comparison group rates.

EQ4: How did EB and monitored student participation in magnet programs 
compare to NonEB peers?48 
This section compares magnet program participation among EBs, monitored students, and 
NonEB49 students. 
As shown in Table 14, 1,883 EBs applied for participation in 2023-24 magnet programs (a decrease 
of 203 EBs over one year),50 amounting to approximately 49 percent of total qualified applicants. The offer 
rate for EB applicants (53%) into at least one magnet program was higher than monitored applicants (35%) 
and NonEBs (49%). Regarding participation, of those who were offered a position, EBs (19%) participated 
in magnet programs at lower rates than monitored students (23%) and NonEBs (30%).
